A categorical semantics for causal structure
Aleks Kissinger, Sander Uijlen

Abstract
. We present a categorical construction for modelling causal structures within a general class of process theories that include the theory of classical probabilistic processes as well as quantum theory. Unlike prior constructions within categorical quantum mechanics, the objects of this theory encode fine-grained causal relationships between subsystems and give a new method for expressing and deriving consequences for a broad class of causal structures. We show that this framework enables one to define families of processes which are consistent with arbitrary acyclic causal orderings. In particular, one can define one-way signalling (a.k.a. semi-causal) processes, non-signalling processes, and quantum n n -combs. Furthermore, our framework is general enough to accommodate recently-proposed generalisations of classical and quantum theory where processes only need to have a fixed causal ordering locally, but globally allow indefinite causal ordering. To illustrate this point, we show that certain processes of this kind, such as the quantum switch, the process matrices of Oreshkov, Costa, and Brukner, and a classical three-party example due to Baumeler, Feix, and Wolf are all instances of a certain family of processes we refer to as SOC n \textrm{SOC}_{n} in the appropriate category of higher-order causal processes. After defining these families of causal structures within our framework, we give derivations of their operational behaviour using simple, diagrammatic axioms.



Apr. 06, 2018Aug. 09, 2019

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 15, Issue 3, Paper 15

1.
Introduction

Broadly, causal structures identify which events or processes taking place across space and time can, in principle, serve as causes or effects of one another. In the field of statistical causal inference, directed acyclic graphs (or generalisations thereof) have been used to capture that fact that certain random variables may have causal influences on others. That is, forcefully changing a certain variable (e.g., whether a patient takes a drug or placebo in randomised trial) may have an effect on another variable (e.g., whether the patient recovers). Intuitively, it may seem impossible to draw such causal conclusions without making such an intervention, however it has been shown in recent years that, under certain assumptions, one can draw causal conclusions from purely observational data
[
33
]
. For instance, the
constraint-based approach
to causal discovery uses conditional independences present in statistical data to iteratively rule out possible causal relationships by removing edges from a (typically very large) directed graph. This sits at the heart of modern, graph-based causal discovery algorithms such as FCI
[
39
]
, and has been successful in a wide variety of real-world applications, including the study of ADHD
[
38
]
, neural connectivity
[
20
]
, gene regulation networks
[
36
]
, and climate change
[
18
]
.

In recent years, it has been asked whether, and to what extent, concepts from classical causal reasoning can be transferred to quantum theory, or more generalised theories of interacting processes. A major obstacle to employing classical techniques comes from the presence of
quantum non-locality
, i.e., the possibility of correlations observed between distant, non-communicating agents which cannot be explained by a classical common cause. Indeed, Wood and Spekkens showed that quantum theory allows correlations which admit no faithful classical causal model
[
42
]
. Roughly speaking, this means that any attempt to naïvely reproduce quantum correlations with a classical causal model will necessarily include spurious causal relationships. Hence, there have been numerous attempts to extend classical causal models to quantum
[
35
,
16
,
2
]
or even more general
[
24
]
models.

In the context of quantum theory, causal relationships between inputs and outputs to quantum processes have been expressed in a variety of ways. Perhaps the simplest are in the form of non-signalling constraints, which guarantee that distant agents are not capable of sending information faster that the speed of light, e.g., to affect each other’s measurement outcomes
[
4
]
. Quantum strategies
[
22
]
and more recently quantum combs
[
5
]
offer a means of expressing more intricate causal relationships, in the form of chains of causally ordered inputs and outputs. Furthermore, it has been shown recently that one can formulate a theory that is locally consistent with quantum theory yet assumes no fixed background causal structure
[
32
]
. Interestingly, such a theory admits
indefinite
causal structures. Namely, it allows one to express processes which inhabit a quantum superposition of causal orders.

Such processes are especially interesting for two reasons. First, indefinite causal structure seems to be an inevitable property of any theory that attempts to combine causal dynamics of general relativity with the irreducible non-determinism of quantum theory
[
23
]
. Hence, indefinite causal structures can provide interesting ‘toy models’ that exhibit quantum gravity-like features. Second, if physically realisable, processes with indefinite causal ordering can be exploited to gain an advantage in comminication problems, such as the ‘guess your neighbour’s input’ game
[
32
]
and computational tasks, such as single-shot quantum channel discrimination
[
8
]
. Perhaps surprisingly, this phenomenon is not unique to quantum processes: it has been shown recently that, in the presence of three or more parties, causal bounds can be violated even within a theory that behaves locally like classical probability theory
[
3
]
. The key ingredient in studying (and varying) causal structures of processes is the development of a coherent theory of
higher order causal processes
. That is, if we treat local agents (or events, laboratories, etc.) as first order causal processes, then the act of composing these processes in a particular causal order should be treated as a second-order process.

This paper aims to represent causal relationships within ‘black box’ processes (which could be classical, quantum, or more general) in a uniform way, and provide some of the first tools for ‘generalised causal reasoning’ which can be applied in both the classical and quantum contexts. To do this, we start from a category
\mathcal{C}
of ‘raw materials’—whose morphisms should be thought of as processes without any causal constraints—and construct a new category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
of first and higher-order processes which are consistent with certain given causal constraints.

For
\mathcal{C}
, we introduce a new kind of category called a
precausal category
, which is a compact closed category with a bit of extra structure enabling us to reason about causal relationships between systems.
Most notably, precausal categories have a special
discarding process
defined on each object, which enables one to say when a process is
causal
, namely when it satisfies the following equation:

{\Phi}
B
A
=
A

This has a clear operational intuition:

If the output of a causal process is discarded, it doesn’t matter which process occurred
.

…or put a slightly different way:

The only influence a causal process has is on its output
.

In particular, causality rules out the possibility of hidden side effects or the ‘backwards flow’ of information from a given process.
While seemingly obvious, the causality condition, originally given by
[
7
]
in the context of operational probabilistic theories, is surprisingly powerful. For instance, it is equivalent to the non-signalling property for joint processes arising from shared correlations
[
13
]
.

It is thus natural to consider the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of all causal processes. However, we take this a step further and consider not only (first-order) causal processes, but also higher-order mappings which preserve certain kinds of causal processes. This yields a
*
-autonomous category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
, into which the category of all first-order causal processes embeds fully and faithfully. We therefore call categories of the form
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]

higher-order causal categories
(HOCCs). Our main examples start from the precausal categories of matrices of positive real numbers and completely positive maps, which will yield HOCCs of higher-order classical stochastic processes and higher-order quantum channels, respectively.

While categorical quantum mechanics
[
1
]
has typically focussed on compact closed categories of quantum processes, we show that this
*
-autonomous structure yields a much richer type system for describing causal relationships between systems. Whereas compact closed categories have only one way to form joint systems, namely
\otimes
,
*
-autonomous categories have two:
\otimes
and
\invamp
. A simple, yet striking example of the difference in these two connectives in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
comes from forming types of processes on a joint system:

\displaystyle(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ \ \leftarrow\ \textrm{non-signalling processes}

\displaystyle(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ \ \leftarrow\ \textrm{all processes}

Using the richer type system afforded by
*
-autonomous categories, we are able to give logical characterisations of many kinds of first- and higher-order causality constraints, and show, for example, when certain constraints imply others.

The paper is structured as follows. In section
2
, we outline the basics of compact closed categories, string diagrams,
*
-autonomous categories, discarding, and (non-)signalling processes. In section
3
, we introduce precausal categories and prove some basic properties, such as the
no time-travel
theorem for precausal categories. In section
4
, we construct the higher-order causal category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
and show that it is
*
-autonomous. In section
5
, we develop various properties of first-order systems, most notably the coincidence of
\otimes
and
\invamp
. In section
6
, we demonstrate the aforementioned dichotomy of
\otimes
and
\invamp
at the level of second-order sytems: namely that
\otimes
can be used to construct a type of non-signalling processes whereas
\invamp
yields all processes. Furthermore, we show that one-way signalling processes can be captured in a HOCC using a generalisation of quantum combs, and processes satisfying arbitrary acyclic causal structures can be captured using pullbacks of combs. In section
7
, we show that HOCCs also enable us to capture bipartite and
n
-partite second-order causal processes. We give several examples which are known to exhibit indefinite causal structure, namely the OCB process from
[
32
]
, the classical tripartite process from
[
3
]
, and an abstract version of the quantum switch defined in
[
8
]
. Finally, we prove using just the structure of
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
that the switch does not admit a causal ordering by reducing to no time-travel.

Related work.
This work was inspired by
[
34
]
, which aims for a uniform description of higher-order
quantum
operations in terms of generalised Choi operators. However, rather than relying on the linear structure of spaces of operators, we work purely in terms of the
*
-autonomous structure and the precausal axioms, which concern the compositional behaviour of discarding processes. The construction of
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
is a variant of the
double gluing construction
used in
[
25
]
to construct models of linear logic. In the language of that paper, our construction consists of building the ‘tight orthogonality category’ induced by a focussed orthogonality on
\{1_{I}\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(I,I)
, and then restricting to objects satisfying the flatness condition in Definition
4.2
.
Since it is
*
-autonomous,
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
indeed gives a model of multiplicative linear logic, enabling us to enlist the aid of linear-logic based tools for proving theorems about causal types. We comment briefly on this in the conclusion. When fixing
\mathcal{C}=\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the category
\textrm{Caus}[\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})]
is closely related to (the finite-dimensional subcategory of) probabilistic coherence spaces, introduced in
[
21
]
and refined in
[
17
]
. The main difference, aside from allowing matrices over infinite sets of indices, is that the latter defines duals with respect to
[0,1]\subseteq\mathcal{\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}(I,I)\cong\mathbb{R}_{+}
rather than
\{1\}
, which allows one to capture sub-normalised probability distributions as well as normalised ones.
Our construction indeed extends to incorporate sub-normalised processes, but understanding the structure of the resulting, larger category and its relationship to the one we define is a subject of future work. It is worth noting however, that the correspondence with coherence spaces does not extend to the quantum case. In particular,
\textrm{Caus}[\textbf{CPM}]
is better behaved than the quantum coherence spaces defined by Gerard in
[
21
]
, as it inherits the quantum tensor product—and hence the usual notions of positivity for states and complete positivity for morphisms—from the underlying category
CPM
.

Acknowledgements.
Both authors are grateful to Paulo Perinotti for his input and sharing a draft version of
[
34
]
. We would also like to thank Časlav Brukner, Matty Hoban, and Sam Staton for useful discussions. This work is supported by the ERC under the European Union’s Seventh Framework Programme (FP7/2007-2013) / ERC grant n
o
320571.

This is an extended version of a conference paper with the same title
[
28
]
. While the overall structure is the same, it has been expanded with additional examples and explanation and provides two new technical contributions. The first is a proof that
Rel
is ‘weakly’ precausal, in that it satisfies all of the precausal axioms except
(\textrm{C5}^{\prime})
. The second and more substantial contribution is the pullback construction in Section
6.2
which is used to capture all acyclic causal structures, rather than just linear ones as in
[
28
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

We work in the context of
symmetric monoidal categories
(SMCs). An SMC consists of a collection of objects
\textrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})
, for every pair of objects,
A,B\in\textrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})
a set
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
of morphisms, associative sequential composition ‘
\circ
’ with units
1_{A}
for all
A\in\textrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})
, associative (up to isomorphism) parallel composition ‘
\otimes
’ for objects and morphisms with unit
I\in\textrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})
, and swap maps
\sigma_{A,B}:A\otimes B\to B\otimes A
, satisfying the usual equations one would expect for composition and tensor product. For simplicity, we furthermore assume
\mathcal{C}
is
strict
, i.e.

(A\otimes B)\otimes C=A\otimes(B\otimes C)\qquad\qquad A\otimes I=A=I\otimes A\vskip 0.0pt

This is no loss of generality since every SMC is equivalent to a strict one. For details,
[
31
]
is a standard reference.

We wish to treat SMCs as theories of physical processes, hence we often refer to objects as
systems
and morphisms as
processes
. We will also extensively use
string diagram
notation for SMCs, where systems are depicted as wires, processes as boxes, and:

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

, a process

is called an

, and

or

. Depicted as string diagrams:

Numbers in an SMC always form a commutative monoid with ‘multiplication’
\circ
and unit the identity morphism
1_{I}
. We typically write
1_{I}
simply as
1
.

2.1.
Compact closed categories and higher-order string diagrams

We will begin with a category
\mathcal{C}
and construct a new category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
of higher-order causal processes. In order to make this construction, we first need a mechanism for expressing higher-order processes.
Compact closure
provides such a mechanism that is convenient within the graphical language and already familiar within the literature on quantum channels, in the guise of the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism.

Definition 2.1
.

An SMC
\mathcal{C}
is called
compact closed
if every object
A
has a
dual
object
A^{*}
. That is, for every
A
there exists morphisms
\eta_{A}:I\to A^{*}\otimes A
and
\epsilon_{A}:A\otimes A^{*}\to I
, satisfying:

(\epsilon_{A}\otimes 1_{A})\circ(1_{A}\otimes\eta_{A})=1_{A}\qquad\qquad(1_{A^{*}}\otimes\epsilon_{A})\circ(\eta_{A}\otimes 1_{A^{*}})=1_{A^{*}}

We refer to
\eta_{A}
and
\epsilon_{A}
as a cup and a cap, denoted graphically as

and

, respectively. In this notation, the equations in Definition
2.1
become:

=
A
A
A
A^{*}

=
A^{*}
A^{*}
A^{*}
A

It is always possible to choose cups and caps in such a way that the canonical isomorphisms
I^{*}\cong I
,
(A\otimes B)^{*}\cong A^{*}\otimes B^{*}
, and
A\cong A^{**}
are all in fact equalities. We will assume this is the case throughout this paper.

Crucially, two morphisms in a free compact closed category are equal if and only if their string diagrams are the same. That is, if one diagram can be continuously deformed into the other while maintaining the connections between boxes. Hence, when we draw a string diagram, we mean
any
composition of boxes via cups, caps, and swaps which yields the given diagram, up to deformation. See
[
37
]
for an overview of string diagram languages for monoidal categories.

Compact closed categories exhibit
process-state duality
,
that is, processes
f:A\to B
are in 1-to-1 correspondence with states
\rho_{f}:I\to A^{*}\otimes B
:

\XKV@sttrue\XKV@plfalse\XKV@testoptc\XKV@setkeys{keycmd->boxmap}{}\,\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to35.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.31319pt\lower-17.98297pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.84 -9.84 h 19.69 v 19.69 h -19.69 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.73436pt}{-2.69096pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{}{}
{}{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(1)

Hence, we treat everything as a ‘state’ in
\mathcal{C}
and write
f:X
as shorthand for
f:I\to X
.
In this notation, states are of the form
\rho:A
, effects
\pi:A^{*}
, and general processes
f:A^{*}\otimes B
.
Furthermore, we won’t require ‘output’ wires to exit upward, and we allow irregularly-shaped boxes.
For example, we can write a process
w:A^{*}\otimes B\otimes C^{*}\otimes D
using ‘comb’ notation:

A
B
C
D
:=
w
A^{*}
B
C^{*}
D
w

(2)

Note that we adopt the convention that an
A
-labelled ‘input’ wire is of the same type as an
A^{*}
-labelled ‘output’.

While both the LHS and the RHS in equation (
2
) are notation for the same process
w
, the LHS is strongly suggestive of a second-order mapping from processes
B\to C
to processes
A\to D
. Composition in this notation simply means applying the appropriate ‘cap’ processes to plug wires together:

\Phi
B
A
D
C
:=
D
A^{*}
w
C^{*}
B
C
B^{*}
\Phi
w

Remark 2.2
.

Since oddly-shaped boxes don’t uniquely fix any ordering of systems with respect to
\otimes
, we will often ‘name’ each system by giving it a unique type and assume systems are permuted via
\sigma
-maps whenever necessary. This is a common practice e.g., in the quantum information literature.

Our key examples of compact closed categories to which we will apply our construction to obtain categories of higher-order causal types,
will be
\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
CPM
, which we introduce now.
They contain stochastic matrices and quantum channels, respectively.

Example 2.3
.

The category
\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
has as objects natural numbers. Morphisms
f:m\to n
are
n\times m
matrices. Composition is given by matrix multiplication:
(g\circ f)_{i}^{j}:=\sum_{k}f_{i}^{k}g_{k}^{j}
, and the tensor is given by
m\otimes n:=mn
and
f\otimes g
is the Kronecker product of matrices:

(f\otimes g)_{ij}^{kl}:=f_{i}^{k}g_{j}^{l}\vskip 0.0pt

Consequently, the tensor unit
I
is the natural number
1
, so that states are column vectors
\rho:1\to n
, effects are row vectors
\pi:n\to 1
, and scalars are positive numbers
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is compact closed with
n=n^{*}
, where cups and caps are given by the Kronecker delta,
\delta_{ij}
,
\delta_{ij}=1
if
i=j
,
\delta_{ij}=0
otherwise. That is:

\eta^{ij}:=\delta_{ij}=:\epsilon_{ij}\vskip 0.0pt

Example 2.4
.

The category
CPM
has as objects the sets of linear operators,
\mathcal{L}(H)
,
\mathcal{L}(K),\ldots
, on finite dimensional complex Hilbert spaces
H,K,\ldots
and morphisms
\Phi:\mathcal{L}(H)\to\mathcal{L}(K)
are completely positive maps (i.e., positive maps which are still positive when tensored with other maps) with the usual composition:
\Phi\circ\Psi(a)=\Phi(\Psi(a))
.

The tensor product on objects satisfies
\mathcal{L}(H)\otimes\mathcal{L}(K)\cong\mathcal{L}(H\otimes K)
, and on morphisms it is given by linear extension of
\Phi\otimes\Psi(a\otimes b)=\Phi(a)\otimes\Psi(b)
.
Consequently, the tensor unit is
I=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}
and a state is a (completely) positive map
\rho:\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{L}(H)
. Identifying such a map with its image on 1, we may say that a state is positive operator in
\mathcal{L}(H)
.
Effects are (un-normalised) quantum effects, i.e., (completely) positive maps
\pi:\mathcal{L}(H)\to\mathbb{C}
, and are of the form:
\pi(a):=\textrm{Tr}(Pa)
, for some positive operator
P
. As with
\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the scalars are
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

CPM
is also compact closed, with cups and caps given by the (un-normalised) Bell state:

\eta=\ket{\Phi_{0}}\!\bra{\Phi_{0}}\qquad\epsilon(\rho)=\textrm{Tr}(\rho\ket{\Phi_{0}}\!\bra{\Phi_{0}})

where
\ket{\Phi_{0}}=\sum_{i}\ket{i}\otimes\ket{i}
. Consequentially,
\mathcal{L}(H)^{*}=\mathcal{L}(H)
and equation (
1
) gives the basis-dependent version, i.e., ‘Choi-style’, of the Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism
[
29
]
.

2.2.
*
-autonomous categories

The biggest convenience of a compact closed structure is also its biggest drawback:
all higher-order structure collapses to first-order structure! For example, if we (temporarily) take
A\mathop{\multimap}B:=A^{*}\otimes B
to be the object whose states correspond to processes from
A
to
B
, we have:

\displaystyle(A\mathop{\multimap}B)\mathop{\multimap}C

\displaystyle:=(A\mathop{\multimap}B)^{*}\otimes C

\displaystyle:=(A^{*}\otimes B)^{*}\otimes C

\displaystyle\cong A^{**}\otimes B^{*}\otimes C

\displaystyle\cong A\otimes B^{*}\otimes C

\displaystyle\cong B^{*}\otimes A\otimes C

\displaystyle=:B\mathop{\multimap}A\otimes C

As we will soon see, there is a pronounced difference between first-order causal processes which we introduce in the next section, and genuinely higher-order causal processes (see Chapter
6
). Hence, we would not expect such a collapse. If we look carefully at the calculation above, we see that things went wrong in the third step above. Because in any compact closed category, we always have
(A\otimes B)^{*}\cong A^{*}\otimes B^{*}
, we are able to distribute
(-)^{*}
over
\otimes
. If we remove this condition, we obtain a new, weaker kind of category:

Definition 2.5
.

A
*
-autonomous category
is a symmetric monoidal category equipped with a full and faithful functor
(-)^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\textrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
such that, by letting:

A\mathop{\multimap}B:=(A\otimes B^{*})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

(3)

there exists a natural isomorphism:

\mathcal{C}(A\otimes B,C)\cong\mathcal{C}(A,B\mathop{\multimap}C)\vskip 0.0pt

(4)

As the notation and the isomorphism (
4
) suggest,
A\mathop{\multimap}B
is the system whose states correspond to processes from
A
to
B
.
Indeed, take
A=I
in (
4
).

Note that the definition we gave for
A\mathop{\multimap}B
differs from the one we gave for compact closed categories. Indeed any compact closed category is
*
-autonomous, where it additionally holds that:

A\otimes B\cong(A^{*}\otimes B^{*})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

(5)

in which case:

A\mathop{\multimap}B:=(A\otimes B^{*})^{*}\cong A^{*}\otimes B^{**}\cong A^{*}\otimes B\vskip 0.0pt

However, in a
*
-autonomous category, the RHS of (
5
) is not
A\otimes B
, but something new, called the ‘par’ of
A
and
B
:

A\invamp B:=(A^{*}\otimes B^{*})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

(6)

This new operation inherits its good behaviour from
\otimes
:

A\invamp(B\invamp C)\cong(A\invamp B)\invamp C\qquad\qquad A\invamp B\cong B\invamp A\vskip 0.0pt

So a compact closed category is just a
*
-autonomous category where
\otimes=\invamp
. However, this little tweak yields a much richer structure of higher-order maps.
We think of
A\otimes B
as the joint state space of
A
and
B
,
whereas
A\invamp B
is like taking the space of maps from
A^{*}
to
B
.
For (first order) state spaces, these are basically the same, but as we go to higher order spaces,
A\invamp B
tends to be much bigger than
A\otimes B
.

We adopt the programmers’ convention that
\otimes
has precedence over
\mathop{\multimap}
and that
\mathop{\multimap}
associates to the right:

A\otimes B\mathop{\multimap}C:=(A\otimes B)\mathop{\multimap}C\quad\quad A\mathop{\multimap}B\mathop{\multimap}C:=A\mathop{\multimap}(B\mathop{\multimap}C)\vskip 0.0pt

Either expression above represents the system whose states are processes with two inputs. Indeed (
4
) implies that
A\otimes B\mathop{\multimap}C\cong A\mathop{\multimap}B\mathop{\multimap}C
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is symmetric, we can re-arrange the inputs at will, i.e.,

A\mathop{\multimap}B\mathop{\multimap}C\cong B\mathop{\multimap}A\mathop{\multimap}C\vskip 0.0pt

(7)

,

(8)

Using this effect, we can define causality as follows:

Definition 2.6
.

For systems
A
and
B
with discarding processes, a process
\Phi:A\to B
is said to be
causal
if:

{\Phi}
B
A
=
A

(9)

Intuitively, causality means that if we disregard the output of a process, it does not matter which process occurred.

Hence
causal states
produce
1
when discarded:

\pi:A\to I
is the identity, there is a unique causal effect for any system, namely discarding itself:

So, causality is precisely the statement that a vector of positive numbers sums to
1
, i.e., forms a probability distribution. Consequentially, the causality equation (
9
) for a process
\Phi
states that each column of
\Phi
must sum to
1
. That is,
\Phi
is a stochastic map.

Example 2.8
.

For
CPM
, discard is the trace. Hence causal states are positive operators with unit trace a.k.a. density operators and causal processes are trace-preserving completely positive maps, a.k.a. quantum channels.

Discarding not only allows us to express when a process is causal,
it also allows us to represent causal relationships between the systems involved.

For example

Definition 2.9
.

A causal process
\Phi:A\otimes B\to A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
is
one-way signalling
with
A
before
B
(written
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}
where
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}:=(A,A^{\prime})
and
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}:=(B,B^{\prime})
)
if there exists a process
\Phi^{\prime}:A\to A^{\prime}
such that

\Phi^{\prime}\!
B
A
=
\Phi
A^{\prime}
B
B^{\prime}
A^{\prime}
A

(10)

It is one-way signalling with
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}
if there exists
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
such that

=
\Phi^{\prime\prime}\!
A
B
A^{\prime}
B^{\prime}
\Phi
B
B^{\prime}

(11)

Such a process is called
non-signalling

if it is both one-way signalling with
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}
and
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}
.

At first, this might seem like curious terminology, since non-signalling processes are a special case of one-way signalling processes. This is because the term ‘one-way signalling’ does not imply that there
is
communication from Alice to Bob, but rather that communication is
only allowed
from Alice to Bob (and not from Bob to Alice). Contrast this with arbitrary causal processes
\Phi:A\otimes B\to A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
, which allow two-way communication in general.

Note that the expressions
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}
and
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}
in Definition
2.9
are not written in terms of individual inputs or outputs of
\Phi
, but rather input/output pairs
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}:=(A,A^{\prime})
and
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}:=(B,B^{\prime})
.
We call such an input/output pair from a given process an
event
.
If we think of an event in operational terms, it corresponds to a single agent giving an input to his or her black box (e.g., making a measurement choice) and then reading the output.

The definition of one-way signalling readily generalises from two events to
n
events:

Definition 2.10
.

A process
\Phi

is
one-way signalling
with
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{1}\preceq\ldots\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{n-1}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{n}
if
=
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\Phi
\Phi^{\prime}
A_{1}^{\prime}
A_{n}^{\prime}
A_{1}
A_{n}
A_{1}
A_{n-1}
A_{n-1}^{\prime}
A_{1}^{\prime}
A_{n}

with
\Phi^{\prime}
one-way signalling with
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{1}\preceq\ldots\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{n-1}
.

Intuitively, this captures that fact that
\Phi
is consistent with the linear causal ordering of events
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{i}=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime})
. Following
[
26
]
, we can extend this from linear causal orderings to arbitrary (acyclic) causal orderings. For a process
\Phi:A_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes A_{n}\to A_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\ldots\otimes A_{n}^{\prime}
, let
\mathcal{O}
be a partially ordered set whose elements are the pairs
\{\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{i}:=(A_{i},A_{i}^{\prime})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, which we call a
causal ordering
for
\Phi
. Then, for any subset of events
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
let
\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E})
be the down-closure of
\mathcal{E}
with respect to the ordering
\preceq
of
\mathcal{O}
, i.e.,

\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E}):=\{e\ |\ \exists e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}\,.\,e\preceq e^{\prime}\}

In particular, this set contains
\mathcal{E}
itself. Furthermore, let
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{E})
and
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
be given as the direct images of the first and second projections, namely all of the inputs in
\mathcal{E}
and all the outputs in
\mathcal{E}
, respectively.

Definition 2.11
.

A process
\Phi:A_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes A_{n}\to A_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\ldots\otimes A_{n}^{\prime}
is consistent with a causal ordering
\mathcal{O}
(written
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}
) if for all subsets
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
, the outputs of
\mathcal{E}
only depend on the inputs of
\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E})
. That is, there exists another process
\Phi^{\prime}
such that:

\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
\Phi
…
…
…
…
=
…
\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
…
…
\Phi^{\prime}

(12)

Note that, for clarity, we have suppressed the symmetry morphisms (i.e., swap maps) re-ordering the inputs and outputs of
\Phi
in (
12
). In particular, this equation should be understood to apply to all sets of inputs
\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}(\mathcal{E}))
and outputs
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
, not just the leftmost ones.

There is a close connection between causality (
2.6
) and consistency with a causal ordering
\mathcal{O}
. Namely, any (acyclic) string diagram has an evident causal ordering on the inputs/outputs of each of the component boxes, where
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}
if and only if there is a forward-directed path from the box with inputs/outputs
(A,A^{\prime})
to the box with inputs/outputs
(B,B^{\prime})
. For example:

\Phi_{2}
\Phi_{4}
\Phi_{3}
\Phi_{5}
\Phi_{1}
B
B^{\prime}
A^{\prime}
A
C^{\prime}
C
D^{\prime}
D
E^{\prime}
E

\leadsto

A
C
B
D
E

It was shown in
[
26
]
that all acyclic diagrams in a category
\mathcal{C}
respect their associated causal ordering if and only if all processes in
\mathcal{C}
satisfy the causality equation (
9
).

In the coming sections, we will show that consistency with a causal ordering can be expressed within the logic of a higher-order causal category. As a consequence, higher-order constraints such as
preserving
processes which are consistent with a causal ordering can also be expressed.

3.
Precausal categories

Precausal categories give a universe of all processes, and provide enough structure for us to identify which of those processes satisfy first-order and higher-order causality constraints. They are defined as compact closed categories satisfying four extra axioms, which involve discarding and its transpose:

:=
A^{*}
A
A

(13)

which we call the (unnormalised)
uniform state
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
precausal category
is a compact closed category
\mathcal{C}
such that:

(\textrm{C1})

\mathcal{C}
has discarding processes for every system, compatible with the monoidal structure as in (
8
).

(\textrm{C2})

For every (non-zero) system
A
, the
dimension
of
A
:

(\textrm{C1})
enables one to talk about causal processes within
\mathcal{C}
.
(\textrm{C2})
enables us to renormalise certain processes to produce causal ones. For example, every non-zero system has at least one causal state, called the
uniform state
. It is obtained by normalising (
13
):
d_{A}^{-1}
:=
A
A

Then:

d_{A}^{-1}
=
A
A
d_{A}^{-1}d_{A}
=
1

(14)

Remark 3.2
.

Note that by ‘non-zero’ in
(\textrm{C2})
, we mean ‘not a zero object’. For our examples, it will be convenient to allow
\mathcal{C}
to have a zero object (e.g. the natural number
0
in
\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and the zero-dimensional Hilbert space in
CPM
), in which case
d_{0}=0
is certainly not invertible.

(\textrm{C3})
says that processes are characterised by their behaviour on causal states. In the following proposition, we will show that
(\textrm{C3})
implies that it suffices to consider only product states to identify a process.

(15)

Proof.

(\textrm{C3})
follows from (
15
) by taking one of the two systems involved to be trivial. Conversely, assume the premise of (
15
). Applying
(\textrm{C3})
one time yields:
\Phi
\rho_{2}
=
\rho_{2}
\Phi^{\prime}

for all causal states
\rho_{2}
. Bending the wire yields:
\Phi
\rho_{2}
=
\rho_{2}
\Phi^{\prime}

Hence we can apply
(\textrm{C3})
a second time. Bending the wire back down gives the result.∎

(\textrm{C4})
is perhaps the least transparent. It says that the only mappings from causal processes to causal processes are ‘circuits with holes’, i.e., those mappings which arise from plugging a causal process into a larger circuit of causal processes. This can equivalently be split into two smaller pieces, which will be helpful in showing
\textbf{Mat}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
CPM
form examples of precausal categories.

Proposition 3.4
.

For a compact closed category
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
(\textrm{C1})
,
(\textrm{C2})
, and
(\textrm{C3})
, condition
(\textrm{C4})
is equivalent to the following two conditions:

(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})

one-way signalling

(\textrm{C5}^{\prime})
:
w

=
\rho^{\prime}
\implies
Lemma
3.6
w
=
\rho
\rho
w

Then, by
(\textrm{C3})
:
w
=
w
=:
w^{\prime}\!

Hence
w
satisfies the premise of
(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})
, up to diagram deformation, where
\Phi:=w
and
\Phi^{\prime}:=w^{\prime}
, which implies that it factors as in
(\textrm{C4})
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Whereas axiom
(\textrm{C5}^{\prime})
is just the restriction of
(\textrm{C4})
to a special case,
(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})
is an abstract version of a familiar result about quantum channels. In the literature, one-way signalling is sometimes called
semi-causal
, whereas the factorisation of
\Phi
into processes
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
in
(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})
is referred to a
semi-localisable
. Whereas it is immediately clear that semi-localisable implies semi-causal, the converse, i.e. condition
(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})
, is non-trivial. It was conjectured for quantum channels by DiVincenzo and independently by Beckman et al
[
4
]
, and proven by Eggeling, Schlingemann, and Werner in
[
19
]
. In the case of classical probabilistic processes,
(\textrm{C4}^{\prime})
can be seen as an instance of the product rule, applied to conditional probabilities of the form
P(A^{\prime}B^{\prime}|AB)
satisfying the conditional independence
P(A^{\prime}|AB)=P(A^{\prime}|A)
. See Appendix
A.2
for details.

Example 3.8
.

Rel

While, as we shall soon see, precausal categories give us a source of processes exhibiting many varieties of definite and indefinite causal structure, the axioms rule out certain, paradoxical causal structures. To see this, we state our first no-go result for a precausal category
\mathcal{C}
.

Theorem 3.10

(No time-travel)
.

No non-trivial system
A
in a precausal category
\mathcal{C}
admits
time travel
.
That is, if there exist systems
B
and
C

\Phi

(16)

then
A\cong I
.

Proof.

For any causal process
\Psi:A\to A
, we can define:
\Phi
A
B
C
A
:=
\Psi
A
A
C
B

which is also a causal process. Then implication (
16
) gives:

\Phi
A
B
C
=
B
=
1
=
A
\Psi

(\textrm{C5}^{\prime})

Note that a special case of Theorem

implies that if for all causal processes

we have
\Phi

=

then

.

4.

\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]

We will now describe our main construction, the category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
of higher-order causal processes for a precausal category
\mathcal{C}
. To motivate this construction, we begin by looking at the properties of the set of causal states for some system
A
:

(17)

In the classical and quantum cases, these form convex subsets of real vector spaces (probability distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and density matrices in
\textrm{SA}_{n}
, the self adjoint
n\times n
operators, respectively). We would like to recapture the fact that this set is suitably closed without referring to convexity, so we appeal to duals instead.

.

For any set of states
c\subseteq\mathcal{C}(I,A)
, we can define the dual set

as follows:

Taking the dual again, we get back to a set of states in
A
. It immediately follows from the definition that
c\subseteq c^{**}
. For the set of all causal states (
17
), we see that furthermore
c=c^{**}
. Assuming this property, which we call
closure
, for all objects will play an important role in obtaining
*
-autonomous structure in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
.

However, this property alone only refers to the compact closed structure of
\mathcal{C}
and not the precausal structure. By definition, the discarding process is contained in the dual
c^{*}
of the set of all causal states. We also showed in equation (
14
) that the transpose of discarding is contained in the set of causal states, up to a scalar (namely
d_{A}^{-1}
). Generalising this to a property that is symmetric in the roles of
c
and
c^{*}
, we obtain the notion of
flatness
.

Definition 4.2
.

A set of states
c\subseteq\mathcal{C}(I,A)
is
closed
if
c=c^{**}
and
flat
if there exist invertible scalars
\lambda,\mu
such that:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\in c^{*}

As we have already noted, the set of all causal states is closed and flat. Many other sets turn out to be closed and flat, including the sets of causal processes and higher-order generalisations thereof. We now use these two properties to define a category which incorporates all of these higher-order types.

Definition 4.3
.

For a precausal category
\mathcal{C}
, the category
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
has as objects pairs:

\bm{A}:=(A,c_{\bm{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(I,A))\vskip 0.0pt

where
c_{\bm{A}}
is closed and flat. A morphism
f:\bm{A}\to\bm{B}
is a morphism
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
such that:

\rho\in c_{\bm{A}}\ \implies\ f\circ\rho\in c_{\bm{B}}\vskip 0.0pt

(18)

We refer to categories of the form
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
for some precausal category
\mathcal{C}
as
higher-order causal categories
(HOCCs).

While condition (
18
) is given in terms of states, closure allows us to equivalently present it in terms of effects or numbers:

Proposition 4.4
.

For objects
\bm{A},\bm{B}
in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
and a morphism
f:A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\rho\in c_{\bm{A}}\implies f\circ\rho\in c_{\bm{B}}

(ii)

\pi\in c_{\bm{B}}^{*}\implies\pi\circ f\in c_{\bm{A}}^{*}

(iii)

\rho\in c_{\bm{A}},\pi\in c_{\bm{B}}^{*}\implies\pi\circ f\circ\rho=1

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) follow immediately from the definition of
(-)^{*}
, so assume (iii) and take any
\rho\in c_{\bm{A}}
. Then for all
\pi\in c_{\bm{B}}^{*}
,
\pi\circ(f\circ\rho)=1
. Hence
f\circ\rho\in c_{\bm{B}}^{**}=c_{\bm{B}}
.
∎

Since a set of states is closed when
c=c^{**}
, it is natural to ask if
(-)^{**}
forms a closure operation, namely if it is idempotent. This is an immediate result of the following:

Lemma 4.5
.

For any set of states
c
we have
c^{*}=c^{***}
.

Proof.

First, note that:

c\subseteq d\ \implies\ d^{*}\subseteq c^{*}.

Applying this to
c\subseteq c^{**}
yields
c^{***}\subseteq c^{*}
.
But then, it is already the case that
c^{*}
is contained in
c^{***}
,
so
c^{***}=c^{*}
.
∎

We will now show that
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
has the structure of a
*
-autonomous category. To do this, we will first define the tensor
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}
. For the sets of states
c_{\bm{A}}
and
c_{\bm{B}}
, we denote the set of all product states as follows:

c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}}\ :=\ \{\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}\ |\ \rho_{1}\in c_{\bm{A}},\ \rho_{2}\in c_{\bm{B}}\}\vskip 0.0pt

Then,
c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}
is the closure of the set of all product states:

c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}:=(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{**}\vskip 0.0pt

Lemma 4.6
.

For any effect
\pi:A^{*}\otimes B^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}
:

\left(\begin{matrix}\ \textrm{\footnotesize$\forall\ \rho\in c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}$}\ .\ \\[2.84526pt]
\ \hbox to43.08pt{\vbox to42.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.53955pt\lower-24.76222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.69 -14.76 L -19.69 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -14.76 L 19.69 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -33.99 L 29.53 -9.89 L -29.53 -9.89 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.58507pt}{-15.40694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

(19)

Proof.

The LHS of (
19
) states that

\pi\in c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}^{*}:=((c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{**})^{*}=(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{***}\vskip 0.0pt

whereas the RHS states that
\pi\in(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{*}
. Hence, (
19
) follows from Lemma
4.5
.
∎

Theorem 4.7
.

\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
is an SMC, with tensor given by:

\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}:=(A\otimes B,c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}})\vskip 0.0pt

and tensor unit
\bm{I}:=(I,\{1\})
.

Proof.

The proof is in the appendix.
∎

Now define objects
\bm{A}^{*}:=(A^{*},c_{\bm{A}^{*}})
in the obvious way, by letting
c_{\bm{A}^{*}}:=c_{\bm{A}}^{*}
. Then

(20)

lifts to a full and faithful functor
(-)^{*}:\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]^{\textrm{op}}\to\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
, where
\bm{A}^{*}:=(A^{*},c_{\bm{A}^{*}})
.

Proof.

The main part is showing that
f^{*}
is again a morphism, but this follows from the definition of the star on sets of states.
A full proof is in the appendix.
∎

We now have enough structure to define
\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}:=(\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}^{*})^{*}
. However, it is enlightening to give an explicit characterisation of the set
c_{\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}}
. This will be no surprise:

.

For objects

:

Proof.

This follows by simplifying:

c_{(\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}^{*})^{*}}=c_{(\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}^{*})}^{*}=(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}^{*}})^{***}=(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}}^{*})^{*}

and noting that
f\in(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}}^{*})^{*}
is precisely the statement given in the lemma.
∎

Theorem 4.10
.

For any precausal category
\mathcal{C}
,
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
is a
*
-autonomous category where
\bm{I}=\bm{I}^{*}
.

Proof.

Since compact closed categories already admit an interpretation for
\mathop{\multimap}
satisfying (
4
), it suffices to show that this isomorphism lifts to
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
. This follows from the application of Lemma
4.6
. The complete proof is in the appendix.
∎

Since
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
is
*
-autonomous, we can also define the ‘par’ of two systems
\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
. Since
\invamp
is related to
\mathop{\multimap}
via
\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}\cong\bm{A}^{*}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}
, Lemma
4.9
also yields an explicit form for
c_{\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}}
by replacing
\bm{A}
with
\bm{A}^{*}
:

c_{\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}}=\left\{\rho:A\otimes B\ \bigg|\ \textrm{\footnotesize$\forall\pi\in c_{\bm{A}}^{*},\xi\in c_{\bm{B}}^{*}\ .\ $}\hbox to43.08pt{\vbox to38.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.53955pt\lower-21.20563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

Note that the process
f:A^{*}\otimes B
has become a bipartite state
\rho:A\otimes B
. That is, the states
\rho:\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
are states which are normalised for all product effects. Symbolically, the two monoidal products are defined as follows:

c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}=(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{**}\qquad c_{\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}}=(c_{\bm{A}}^{*}\otimes c_{\bm{B}}^{*})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

One can easily check that
(c_{\bm{A}}^{*}\otimes c_{\bm{B}}^{*})\subseteq(c_{\bm{A}}\otimes c_{\bm{B}})^{*}
. Thus, since
(-)^{*}
reverses subset inclusions, that

c_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}\subseteq c_{\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}}
.
Consequently, the identity
1_{A\otimes B}
in
\mathcal{C}
lifts to a canonical embedding
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\hookrightarrow\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
. This agrees with the intuition given in Section
3
that
\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
is the ‘larger’ of the two ways to combine
A
and
B
into a joint system.

Remark 4.11
.

A
*
-autonomous category with coherent isomorphism
I\cong I^{*}
, such as
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
, is also called an ISOMIX category
[
10
]
. This innocent-looking extra condition actually gives a great deal more structure. For instance, even though we showed it concretely, the existence of a canonical morphism
A\otimes B\to A\invamp B
is implied purely from this extra structure.

Rather than thinking of
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
as a totally new category constructed from
\mathcal{C}
, it is useful to think of it as endowing the processes in
\mathcal{C}
with a much richer type system. As in the compact closed case, it suffices to consider only processes out of
\bm{I}
and we use
\rho:\bm{X}
as shorthand for
\rho:\bm{I}\to\bm{X}
. However, unlike before, we will often use a statement of the form
\rho:\bm{X}
as a
proposition
about a state
\rho\in\mathcal{C}(I,X)
.

Proposition 4.12
.

For a system
\bm{X}=(X,c_{\bm{X}})
in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
and a state
\rho\in\mathcal{C}(I,X)
,
\rho:\bm{X}
if and only if
\rho\in c_{\bm{X}}
.

Proof.

Since
1
is the unique state in
c_{\bm{I}}
, the result follows immediately from the definition of morphism in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
:

\rho:\bm{X}\ \iff\ \rho\circ 1\in c_{\bm{X}}\ \iff\ \rho\in c_{\bm{X}}

From now on, we will use
\rho:\bm{X}
and
\rho\in c_{\bm{X}}
interchangeably without further comment. We will also mix the graphical notation with the type-theoretic. So, for instance, if we write:

\hbox to54.35pt{\vbox to106.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 46.73573pt\lower-60.44928pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-24.89616pt}{-46.23573pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ :\ \bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{C})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{D}\vskip 0.0pt

this should be interpreted as a morphism in
\mathcal{C}(I,A^{*}\otimes B\otimes C^{*}\otimes D)
, along with the assertion that this morphism has type
\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{C})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{D}
. In particular, diagrams always depict
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms, as opposed to
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
-morphisms, so there is no ambiguity about whether parallel composition means
\otimes
or
\invamp
.

Furthermore, if we state that two types are isomorphic without giving the isomorphism explicitly, it should be understood that the underlying isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
is just the identity, up to a possible permutation of systems. In particular,
\bm{X}\cong\bm{Y}
implies that
\rho:\bm{X}
if and only if
\rho:\bm{Y}
.

5.
First order systems

For any precausal category
\mathcal{C}
, we can always form the SMC of (first-order) causal processes
\mathcal{C}_{c}
by restricting just to those processes satisfying the causality equation (
9
). Since
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
is supposed to contain first and higher-order causal processes, one would naturally expect
\mathcal{C}_{c}
to embed in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
.

,
\Phi\in\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is a morphism from
\bm{A}
to
\bm{B}
if and only if for every
\pi\in c_{\bm{B}}^{*}
,
\pi\circ\Phi\in c_{\bm{A}}^{*}
. Since both of these sets of effects only contain discarding, this reduces to the causality equation (
9
).
∎

Furthermore, first-order systems are closed under
\otimes

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}_{\!\!A}\}^{*})\qquad\qquad f\mapsto f\vskip 0.0pt

Hence, the full sub-category of first-order systems and processes behaves as expected; it is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}_{c}
. Perhaps a more surprising corollary to Proposition
5.3
is the following.

∎

So for first-order systems, there is really only one way to form the ‘joint system’. However, we will now see that for higher-order systems, this is very much not the case.

6.
Higher-order systems with definite causal order

While it is important that the category of causal processes embeds fully and faithfully in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
when one restricts to first-order systems, the chief interest of
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
are its higher-order systems.
The goal of this section is to show that certain collections of maps fit nicely within the developed type theory.

The first non-trivial second-order system that it is natural to consider is
\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}
for first-order systems
\bm{A}
,
\bm{B}
. The isomorphism (
4
) for
*
-autonomous categories restricts to:

\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}](\bm{A},\bm{B})\cong\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}](\bm{I},\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\vskip 0.0pt

so ‘states’
\Phi:\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}
are in bijective correspondence with morphisms from
\bm{A}
to
\bm{B}
in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
. That is, they are precisely the causal processes from
\bm{A}
to
\bm{B}
.

Now, starting from first-order systems
\bm{A},\bm{A}^{\prime},\bm{B},\bm{B}^{\prime}
, we have two ways to form the ‘joint system’ from
\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}
and
\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}
, via
\otimes
and
\invamp
. Before we characterise these systems, we examine the dual of a second-order system.

If we take a process
w:(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})^{*}
in the dual system, we know from
(\textrm{C4})
that that
w
must split into two pieces, a causal state and the discard map. In fact, using flatness, we can strengthen this condition by only requiring
\bm{B}
to be first-order.

.

\bm{X}

\bm{B}

and
\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}

Theorem 6.2
.

For first-order systems
\bm{A},\bm{A}^{\prime},\bm{B},\bm{B}^{\prime}
, a process

\Phi
A
B
B^{\prime}
A^{\prime}

\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
if and only if it is causal and non-signalling.

Then we can transform to an equivalent type as follows:

\displaystyle(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{I})

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes\bm{B}^{*}

\displaystyle\cong((\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})^{*}\invamp\bm{B})^{*}

\displaystyle\cong((\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

Hence, by Lemma
6.1
,

Therefore
\Phi:((\bm{A}\multimap\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}\multimap\bm{B}^{\prime}))^{**}=(\bm{A}\multimap\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}\multimap\bm{B}^{\prime})

The proof above also generalises straightforwardly to show that a process is
n
-paritite non-signalling, i.e., for all
i
:
…
…
A^{\prime}_{1}
A^{\prime}_{n}
\Phi^{\prime}
A_{i}
A_{1}
A_{n}
…
…
A_{1}
A_{i}

A^{\prime}_{n}
A_{n}
\Phi
…

A^{\prime}_{i}
A^{\prime}_{i-1}
A^{\prime}_{i+1}
=

if and only if:

\Phi:(\bm{A}_{1}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{1}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{A}_{2}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{2}^{\prime})\otimes\ldots\otimes(\bm{A}_{n}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{n}^{\prime})\vskip 0.0pt

Theorem 6.3
.

For first-order systems
\bm{A},\bm{A}^{\prime},\bm{B},\bm{B}^{\prime}
, a process
\Phi
is of type
(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})
if and only if it is causal. That is:

(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})\cong\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{\prime}\vskip 0.0pt

Proof.

We rely on the relationship between
\mathop{\multimap}
and
\invamp
:

\displaystyle(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}^{*}\invamp\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{*}\invamp\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{A}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{*})^{*}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}\vskip 0.0pt

Then, since
\bm{A}^{\prime}
and
\bm{B}^{\prime}
are first-order,
\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}\cong\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{\prime}
, which completes the proof.
∎

So
(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})
forms the joint system consisting of
all
causal processes from
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}
to
\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{\prime}
, including the signalling ones, e.g.

Hence,
\otimes
and
\invamp
represent two extremes by which
\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}
and
\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}
can be combined, namely by requiring them to be non-signalling or imposing no non-signalling conditions. In the next section, we will see how to recover types that live in between these two extremes: the one-way signalling processes.

The one-way signalling equation (
10
) then becomes:

A
B
B^{\prime}
A^{\prime}
w
=
w^{\prime}\!
A^{\prime}
B
A

(23)

Now, by (
7
) we have:

\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}\cong(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}\vskip 0.0pt

So, for
w
to be a member of the above type, it must send all causal processes
\Psi:\bm{A}^{\prime}\multimap\bm{B}
to causal processes. This immediately follows from (
23
):
w
=
w^{\prime}\!
\Phi
\Phi
=
w^{\prime}\!
=

Conversely, if
w
sends causal processes to causal processes, it factorises as in
(\textrm{C4})
, which implies (
23
):

w
=
\Phi_{2}
\Phi_{1}
\Phi_{1}
=

Hence, one-way signalling admits a more general class of processes than (two-way) non-signalling.

Remark 6.5
.

One can show the embedding of non-signalling processes into one-way signalling processes purely at the level of types by relying on the
linear distributivity
property of
*
-autonomous categories
[
11
]
. Namely, in any
*
-autonomous category, there exists a canonical mapping:

(\bm{X}\invamp\bm{Y})\otimes\bm{Z}\to\bm{X}\invamp(\bm{Y}\otimes\bm{Z})\vskip 0.0pt

We can use this to construct an embedding of non-signalling processes into one-way signalling processes:

\displaystyle(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{A}^{*}\invamp\bm{A}^{\prime})\otimes(\bm{B}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\bm{A}^{*}\invamp(\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes(\bm{B}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\to\bm{A}^{*}\invamp(\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{*})\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}\vskip 0.0pt

Similarly, we can use the embedding
\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y}\to\bm{X}\invamp\bm{Y}
noted in section
4
to show that one-way signalling processes embed in the type
(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})
of all processes.

\displaystyle\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}^{*}\invamp((\bm{A}^{\prime})^{*}\invamp\bm{B})^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\bm{A}^{*}\invamp(\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{*})\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to\bm{A}^{*}\invamp\bm{A}^{\prime}\invamp\bm{B}^{*}\invamp\bm{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime})\invamp(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})

Following
[
34
]
, we generalise from 2-party, one-way signalling processes to
n
-party processes by recursively defining a the type of
n
-
combs
.

Definition 6.6
.

The
n
-combs

C_{n}
are defined by

•

C_{0}=\bm{I}
,

•

C_{i+1}=\bm{B}_{-i}\multimap C_{i}\multimap\bm{B}_{i+1}

.

A 1-comb has type
\bm{B}_{0}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{I}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{1}\cong\bm{B}_{0}\multimap\bm{B}_{1}
, so it is just a causal process. For higher combs, the ‘
-i
’ is employed to maintain the left-to-right order of indices. For example, a 3-comb has type:

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:\bm{B}_{-2}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{B}_{-1}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{B}_{0}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{1})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{2})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{3}\vskip 0.0pt

When necessary, we rename
\bm{A}_{i}:=\bm{B}_{2i-n-1}
and
\bm{A}_{i}^{\prime}:=\bm{B}_{2i-n}
to obtain e.g.,

\bm{A}_{1}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}_{1}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}_{2}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{2}^{\prime})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{3})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{3}^{\prime}\vskip 0.0pt

This recursive definition carries the following intuition. If we think of an
n
comb as a communication protocol with
n
input/output steps, then an
(n+1)
-comb is an
(n+1)
step communication protocol, namely something which takes an initial input, then runs an
n
-step communication protocol, and produces a final output. Hence, we can represent the overall process of two agents running a communication protocol by plugging together Alice’s
n
-comb and Bob’s
(n-1)
-comb:

We give an alternative characterisation for combs in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
, which will relate to one-way signalling processes.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
n
-comb
w:C_{n}
, discarding the output
\bm{A}^{\prime}_{n}
separates as follows, for some
w^{\prime}
:

w
=
w^{\prime}
…
…

(24)

w

Then:

\displaystyle C_{n-1}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{I}\cong C_{n-1}^{*}

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{B}_{-(n-2)}\mathop{\multimap}C_{n-2}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{n-1})^{*}

\displaystyle\cong(\bm{B}_{-(n-2)}\otimes C_{n-2}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}_{n-1})^{*}\vskip 0.0pt

Hence by Lemma
6.1
, in particular equation (
22
), we obtain:
w
\rho
\vdots
=
\rho
\vdots
w

The result then follows from enough causal states.
∎

Note that we haven’t actually said that
w^{\prime}
is itself an
(n-1)
-comb. We will show this now.

Theorem 6.8
.

w
is an
n
-comb, i.e.,
w:C_{n}
, if and only if it separates as in equation (
24
) for some
w^{\prime}:C_{n-1}
.

Proof.

By induction. For
n=1
the theorem is true because a
0
-comb is always
\bm{I}
.
Suppose the theorem is true for
n
.
Let
w
be an
(n+1)
-comb. We need to show that
w^{\prime}
is an
n
-comb.
So let
y
be any
(n-1)
-comb. Then, if we form the process:

\vdots
y

(25)

then clearly discarding the top output results in an
(n-1)
comb (namely
y
) and a discard.
So by the induction hypotheses, (
25
) is an
n
-comb. Therefore we have
\vdots
\vdots
w
y
=
\vdots
w^{\prime}
y
\vdots
=
=
\vdots
y
w^{\prime}
\vdots
(*)
(**)

where
(*)
follows from the definition of
(n+1)
-comb and
(**)
is Lemma
6.7
. Hence
w^{\prime}
sends any
(n-1)
-comb to a causal map, so
w^{\prime}
is itself an
n
-comb.

Conversely, let
w^{\prime}
in equation (
24
) be an
n
-comb, and take any
n
-comb
y
.
Then by the induction hypothesis, discarding the top output of
y
separates as discarding and an
(n-1)
-comb
y^{\prime}
. Hence:
\vdots
\vdots
w
y
=
\vdots
w^{\prime}
\vdots
y
\vdots
w^{\prime}
\vdots
y^{\prime}
=
=

so
w
is an
(n+1)
-comb.
∎

Hence,
n
-combs can be characterised inductively in exactly the same way as
n
-party one-way signalling processes. Since
1
-combs are just causal processes, the following is immediate.

Corollary 6.9
.

For first order systems
\bm{A}_{1},\bm{A}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\bm{A}_{n},\bm{A}^{\prime}_{n}
, a map
w:A_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes A_{n}\to A^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes A^{\prime}_{n}
is one-way signalling (
\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{1}\preceq\ldots\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{A}}_{n}
) if and only if it is of type
\bm{A}_{1}\multimap(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{1}\multimap(\ldots)\multimap\bm{A}_{n})\multimap\bm{A}^{\prime}_{n}
.
That is, it is an
n
-comb.

Stated in terms of consistency with causal orderings, as defined in section
2.3
, the above corollary says that
\Phi:\bm{A}_{1}\multimap(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{1}\multimap(\ldots)\multimap\bm{A}_{n})\multimap\bm{A}^{\prime}_{n}
if and only if:

Proposition
3.9
, then generalises the characterisation theorem for quantum combs in
[
6
]
to
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
for any precausal
\mathcal{C}
: an
n
-comb always factors as a sequence of ‘memory channels’, i.e., a composition of causal processes of the form:
w
=
…
\Phi_{1}
\Phi_{2}
\Phi_{n}
…

6.2.
Directed acyclic causal orders via pullback

We now extend from types which express consistency with arbitrary, linear causal orderings to all acyclic causal orderings. To do so, we first give a new characterisation of the consistency relation
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}
defined in section
2.3
in terms of all of the total orders refining the partial order
\mathcal{O}
. Recall the following standard definition:

Definition 6.10
.

For a partial order
\mathcal{O}
, a
totalisation

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is a total order with the same elements as
\mathcal{O}
such that
e\preceq_{\mathcal{O}}e^{\prime}\implies e\preceq_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}e^{\prime}
.

Totalisations are not unique. For example, a typical ‘common cause’ ordering admits two possible totalisations:

A
C
B

\leadsto

A
B
C

and

B
C
A

corresponding to imposing the (extraneous) causal orderings
\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{C}}
or
\textsf{\footnotesize{C}}\preceq\textsf{\footnotesize{B}}
.
Its important to note that causal constraints come from the
absence
of edges in the Hasse diagrams (i.e., diagrams to visualize a partial order) above, rather than the presence. That is, the absence of an edge between
B
and
C
in the leftmost diagram asserts the independence of the output of
B
from the input of
C
and vice-versa. Hence, consistency with
\mathcal{O}
implies consistency with any totalisation of
\mathcal{O}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For a process
\Phi
, a causal ordering
\mathcal{O}
, and a totalisation
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{O}
, we have
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}\implies\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}
. Then, for any subset
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
, there exists a process
\Phi^{\prime}
such that:

\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
\Phi
…
…
…
…
=
…
\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
…
…
\Phi^{\prime}

(26)

But since
e\preceq_{\mathcal{O}}e^{\prime}\implies e\preceq_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}e^{\prime}
, we have
\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E})
. Hence (
26
) implies that
\Phi
factors as required by Definition
2.11
:

\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
\Phi
…
…
…
…
=
…
\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
…
…
\Phi^{\prime}
…

Therefore
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.
∎

Combining this with its converse yields the following theorem.

Theorem 6.12
.

For a process
\Phi
and a causal ordering
\mathcal{O}
,
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}
if and only if, for every totalisation
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{O}
,
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
follows immediately from Lemma
6.11
.
For
(\Leftarrow)
, let
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
be any subset.
Split
\mathcal{O}
into two parts,
\mathcal{O}_{1}:=\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E})

and
\mathcal{O}_{2}:=\mathcal{O}\backslash\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E})
.
Define a total ordering
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}

by requiring that every element in
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is below every element in
\mathcal{O}_{2}

and taking any totalization on
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}
.
This order refines
\mathcal{O}
because
\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E})
is downward-closed,
and by construction
\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E})=\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E})
.
Hence
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
implies:

\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
\Phi
…
…
…
…
=
…
\pi_{1}(\textbf{past}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{E}))
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E})
…
…
\Phi^{\prime}

Since we can find a suitable totalisation to give the equation above for any subset
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
, we have
\Phi\vDash\mathcal{O}
.
∎

Hence, we can always replace consistency with a causal ordering with consistency with all of its totalisations, i.e., all of the linear orders which refine it. For example:

(27)

We already showed in the previous section that we can capture any linear ordering via combs. Hence, to capture any causal ordering, we only need to be able to take conjunctions of linear causal orders. This can be accomplished via
intersections
of types, much like those defined in
[
34
]
for the quantum case.

The types on the RHS of (
27
) are, respectively:

\bm{X}:=\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{B}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{C})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{C}^{\prime}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\bm{X}^{\prime}:=\bm{A}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{A}^{\prime}\mathop{\multimap}(\bm{C}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{C}^{\prime})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B})\mathop{\multimap}\bm{B}^{\prime}

Our goal now is to form the intersection
\bm{X}\cap\bm{X}^{\prime}
, which will consist precisely of those causal processes consistent with the causal ordering given by
\bm{X}
and the one given by
\bm{X}^{\prime}
. Categorically, intersections are given by pullback, so our first goal will be to find an object in which both
\bm{X}
and
\bm{X}^{\prime}
embed. Since combs are special cases of causal processes, we can embed both
\bm{X}
and
\bm{X}^{\prime}
into the system of
all
causal processes of the appropriate type, namely:

\bm{X}\hookrightarrow(\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\otimes\bm{C}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{B}^{\prime}\otimes\bm{C}^{\prime})\hookleftarrow\bm{X}^{\prime}

In fact, we can always find such embeddings for any system built inductively from first-order systems:

Theorem 6.13
.

Any object
\bm{X}
built inductively from first order systems has a canonical embedding of the form:

e:\bm{X}\rightarrow(\bm{A}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bm{A}_{m}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bm{A}_{n}^{\prime})

for some first-order systems
\bm{A}_{1},\ldots,\bm{A}_{m},\bm{A}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{A}_{n}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the underlying
\mathcal{C}
-morphism is just a permutation of systems.

The proof is a straightforward application of the embedding
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\hookrightarrow\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
and the special property of first order systems that
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}\cong\bm{A}\invamp\bm{B}
. It is given explicitly in the appendix.

We will construct
\bm{X}\cap\bm{X}^{\prime}
essentially in terms of a set-theoretic intersection of their associated states
c_{\bm{X}}
and
c_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}
. For that, we we make use of the following lemma.

.

and

be sets of states for the same object

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.26 5.15 L -7.26 5.15 M 4.84 7.87 L -4.84 7.87 M 2.42 10.6 L -2.42 10.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{0.75}{-0.75}{0.0}{0.0pt}{5.69046pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,}\in(c\cap d)^{*}
and
c\cap d
is flat.

For closure, first note that any set of states is contained in its double dual, so we have
c\cap d\subseteq(c\cap d)^{**}
.
For the converse,
c\cap d\subseteq c
implies
c^{*}\subseteq(c\cap d)^{*}
and similarly
d^{*}\subseteq(c\cap d)^{*}
. Hence,
c^{*}\cup d^{*}\subseteq(c\cap d)^{*}
, so
(c\cap d)^{**}\subseteq(c^{*}\cup d^{*})^{*}
. It therefore suffices to show that
(c^{*}\cup d^{*})^{*}\subseteq c\cap d
. This follows from the fact that
c^{*}\subseteq c^{*}\cup d^{*}
, so
(c^{*}\cup d^{*})^{*}\subseteq c^{**}=c
and similarly
(c^{*}\cup d^{*})^{*}\subseteq d^{**}=d
.
∎

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
\bm{X},\bm{X}^{\prime}
be a pair of objects with canonical embeddings
e,f
into a fixed system
\bm{Y}:=\bm{A}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bm{A}_{m}\mathop{\multimap}\bm{A}_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bm{A}_{n}^{\prime}
,
as in Theorem
6.13
. Then there exists an object
\bm{X}\cap\bm{X}^{\prime}
and morphisms
p_{1},p_{2}
in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
making the following pullback:

\textstyle{\bm{X}\cap\bm{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\bm{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\bm{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\bm{Y}}

(28)

Proof.

Let
\bm{Y}:=(Y,c_{\bm{Y}})
and define the following two sets of states for
\bm{Y}
:

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{\bm{X}}

\displaystyle:=\{e\circ\rho\,|\,\rho\in c_{\bm{X}}\}

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\{f\circ\rho\,|\,\rho\in c_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}\}

Since
e
and
f
are just permutations of systems, it is straightforward to show that both of these sets are flat, closed, and both contain
\lambda\scalebox{0.7}{\,\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to17.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.20659pt\lower-8.0592pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

for some fixed

. Hence, applying Lemma

. It is straightforward to check that

It only remains to show that, for any

and

, there is a unique mediating morphism

\textstyle{\bm{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\,z\,}
\textstyle{\bm{X}\cap\bm{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{e^{-1}}
\textstyle{\bm{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\bm{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\bm{Y}}

Since
e
and
f
are isomorphisms, the only possibility is
z:=e\circ g=f\circ h
. So, it suffices to show that
z
is a morphism in
\textrm{Caus}[\mathcal{C}]
. For any
\rho\in c_{\bm{Z}}
,
g\circ\rho\in c_{\bm{X}}
, so
z\circ\rho=e\circ g\circ\rho\in\overline{c}_{\bm{X}}
. Similarly,
z\circ\rho=f\circ h\circ\rho\in\overline{c}_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}
. Hence
z\circ\rho\in\overline{c}_{\bm{X}}\cap\overline{c}_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}
, which completes the proof.
∎
